Node/npm postinstall spawning AI coding agent CLI (s1ngularity execution chain) AIInstallation High
Detects the s1ngularity-specific parent/child anomaly where a Node.js or npm process directly spawns the `claude`, `gemini`, or `q` binary — the execution shape produced by the `spawnSync` call inside the malicious `telemetry.js` postinstall script. Legitimate AI-CLI usage is started by a human shell, not by an npm lifecycle hook. Rationale: Article shows `spawnSync(cliChecks[name].cmd, args, ...)` inside `telemetry.js` (a Node script registered as the npm postinstall hook), so the malware execution path always presents as `node → claude|gemini|q`. Developers driving these CLIs interactively spawn them from a shell, making the npm-parent shape itself a high-fidelity differentiator without needing to see the bypass flag. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/nrwl/nx/security/advisories/GHSA-cxm3-wv7p-598c • https://www.kodemsecurity.com/resources/vulnerability-alert-s1ngularity-malicious-nx-npm-packages • https://bluefire-redteam.com/nx-s1ngularity-supply-chain-attack-what-you-need-to-know-and-how-to-protect-your-organization/
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1546.016 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Custom Node-based wrapper around an AI CLI used as a developer productivity helper
  • VSCode/Cursor extensions invoking Claude Code through a node helper process
  • CI job that legitimately calls `q chat` from a Node script for AI code-review automation
  • Tooling that uses `npx -p some-pkg claude` as an installation harness
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("cursor","vscode-server","@anthropic-ai/claude-code") and not(InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("node_modules/nx","telemetry.js","postinstall")))
Response runbook
1) Triage: capture the parent's command line — confirm it references `node_modules/nx`, `telemetry.js`, or a lifecycle hook name (`postinstall`, `preinstall`). Pull the SHA256 of the parent Node script and compare against malicious Nx versions (20.9.0–20.12.0, 21.5.0–21.8.0). 2) Contain: isolate host; kill the Node process tree; quarantine the npm cache for the user (`~/.npm/_cacache`) and the user's `node_modules` directory. Rotate npm token, GitHub PAT, cloud and AI vendor keys. 3) Hunt: enumerate all hosts that installed Nx in the 4-hour publishing window (26–27 Aug 2025 UTC) via package-lock.json scanning or SBOM; query for sibling /tmp/inventory.txt writes and outbound api.github.com calls from `node` in the same session.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node","npm","npx") or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm-cli.js","node_modules/nx","postinstall","telemetry.js")
| where FileName in~ ("claude","gemini","q")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, ChildBin=FileName, ChildCmd=ProcessCommandLine, ChildPath=FolderPath, ParentBin=InitiatingProcessFileName, ParentCmd=InitiatingProcessCommandLine, ParentPath=InitiatingProcessFolderPath, GrandparentBin=InitiatingProcessParentFileName
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where ActingProcessName matches regex @"(?i)/(node|npm|npx)$" or ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm-cli.js","node_modules/nx","postinstall","telemetry.js")
| where TargetProcessName matches regex @"(?i)/(claude|gemini|q)$"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine

Node process creating GitHub repo via api.github.com (s1ngularity exfil channel) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts for the s1ngularity exfiltration step — a local Node.js process making outbound HTTPS to api.github.com to POST /user/repos and PUT contents/results.b64 into an attacker-controlled `s1ngularity-repository*` repo using a harvested GitHub token. Pairs with the inventory.txt write or the AI-CLI invocation as a multi-stage signal. Rationale: The article's exfil code path is `githubRequest('/user/repos','POST',...)` followed by `githubRequest('/repos/{full}/contents/results.b64','PUT',...)` — both hit `api.github.com` from the same Node process that already wrote `/tmp/inventory.txt`. The temporal join with the inventory file write is what turns a noisy `node→api.github.com` signal into a high-fidelity exfil detection. Cross-checked against: • https://www.wiz.io/blog/s1ngularity-supply-chain-attack • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/supply-chain-security-alert-popular-nx-build-system-package-compromised-with-data-stealing-malware • https://thehackernews.com/2025/08/malicious-nx-packages-in-s1ngularity.html
ATT&CKT1567.001 T1071.001
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic Endpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate `gh`/`octokit` calls from a Node CI job that also happens to touch /tmp
  • Local dev tools (`semantic-release`, `release-please`, `lerna-publish`) hitting api.github.com from node during a release
  • Renovate/Dependabot-style automation running from a workstation Node context
  • CodeQL or other static-analysis agents pushing results to GitHub from Node
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has_any ("semantic-release","release-please","renovate","dependabot","actions/checkout") and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !contains "github-actions"
Response runbook
1) Triage: enumerate GitHub repos owned by the user via authenticated `gh repo list --visibility public` and grep for `s1ngularity-repository*`; pull the inventory.txt content (it lists every exposed credential file) to scope exposure. 2) Contain: immediately revoke the GitHub PAT/OAuth token used (audit log `git.user.token.revoke` event), make the s1ngularity repos private then delete after preservation, isolate the host, and rotate every secret listed in inventory.txt. 3) Hunt: search GitHub Enterprise audit logs / CommonSecurityLog for repo-create events naming `s1ngularity-repository*` across the org; pivot to other developers who installed Nx within the 4-hour malicious-version window; check OfficeActivity / AAD AuditLogs for any GitHub-OAuth-app authorisation in the same window.
let StagingHosts = DeviceFileEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where FolderPath startswith "/tmp" and FileName in~ ("inventory.txt","inventory.txt.bak")
    | summarize StageTime = min(Timestamp) by DeviceId, DeviceName;
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "node"
| where RemoteUrl has "api.github.com" or RemoteIP in ("140.82.112.0/20","140.82.121.0/24")
| join kind=inner StagingHosts on DeviceId
| where Timestamp between (StageTime - 2m .. StageTime + 30m)
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName=InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort, StageTime
| order by Timestamp desc
let StagingHosts = ImFileEvent
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where TargetFilePath in~ ("/tmp/inventory.txt","/tmp/inventory.txt.bak")
    | summarize StageTime = min(TimeGenerated) by DvcHostname;
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where DstHostname has "api.github.com"
| where SrcUsername != "" 
| join kind=inner StagingHosts on $left.DvcHostname == $right.DvcHostname
| where TimeGenerated between (StageTime - 2m .. StageTime + 30m)
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, SrcUsername, DstHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber, NetworkBytes, StageTime
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Crypto-Wallet Keystore Access by Non-Wallet Process
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d02
status: experimental
description: |
    Stealers / wallet drainers read keystore files from MetaMask,
    Exodus, Atomic, Phantom, Bitcoin, Electrum, Ethereum-cli wallets.
    Filtering out the legitimate wallet binaries leaves only attacker
    activity.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1005/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1005
    - attack.collection
logsource:
    category: file_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_path:
        TargetFilename|contains:
            - '\Ethereum\keystore\'
            - '\Bitcoin\'
            - '\Exodus\'
            - '\Electrum\wallets\'
            - '\MetaMask\'
            - '\Phantom\'
            - '\Atomic\Local Storage\'
    filter_legit:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\MetaMask.exe'
            - '\Exodus.exe'
            - '\Atomic.exe'
            - '\electrum.exe'
            - '\Bitcoin.exe'
            - '\Phantom.exe'
    condition: selection_path and not filter_legit
falsepositives:
    - Backup software
level: high

title: Infostealer - Non-Browser Process Accessing Browser Credential Stores
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d01
status: experimental
description: |
    Stealers (RedLine, Lumma, Vidar, Atomic) read Login Data / cookies
    SQLite databases from Chrome/Edge/Firefox profile directories. Any
    non-browser process touching these files is a high-fidelity signal.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1539/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1555/003/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1539
    - attack.t1555.003
    - attack.credential-access
logsource:
    category: file_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_path:
        TargetFilename|contains:
            - '\Google\Chrome\User Data\'
            - '\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\'

        TargetFilename|endswith:

            - '\chrome.exe'

            - '\opera.exe'

level: high

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
Email attachment opened from external sender Delivery High
Attachment delivery + execution by recipient — most common malware initial access.
ATT&CKT1566.001 T1204.002
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Endpoint.Processes EmailAttachmentInfo DeviceProcessEvents
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MalAttachments = EmailAttachmentInfo
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | project NetworkMessageId, RecipientEmailAddress,
              AttachmentFileName = FileName, AttachmentSHA256 = SHA256;
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                      "mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
| join kind=inner MalAttachments on $left.AccountUpn == $right.RecipientEmailAddress
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, AttachmentFileName, AttachmentSHA256
// Sentinel doesn't have an attachment-hash table. Best approximation:
// OfficeActivity Send/Receive activity + correlate to host process spawn.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MailWithAttach = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive")
    | where isnotempty(MessageId)
    | project EmailTime = TimeGenerated, MailboxOwnerUPN, Sender,
              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName endswith "OUTLOOK.EXE"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
        from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
        where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
          AND Processes.process_name IN ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
        by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
     | rename Processes.user as user]

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.

ATT&CKT1190 T1195.002 T1566
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children

Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

rundll32.exe loading node-gyp.dll dropped by Scavenger-infected npm postinstall (CVE-2025-54313) AIInstallation High
Detects the smoking-gun payload of the eslint-config-prettier / Scavenger Stealer supply-chain compromise: an npm install context (node.exe / npm / yarn / pnpm executing install.js) spawning rundll32.exe to load node-gyp.dll. Legitimate node-gyp is a Node.js native module — it is NEVER loaded via rundll32 in normal use, so the combination is a deterministic IOC. Rationale: Pivot is the deterministic install.js → rundll32.exe node-gyp.dll chain documented in the Snyk article and the CVE-2025-54313 description, with the parent-process narrowed to npm-family binaries. Cross-checked against Invoke-RE, SafeDep, StepSecurity and the NVD CVE record — all four describe the same rundll32 + node-gyp.dll command construction. Cross-checked against: • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-54313 • https://invokere.com/posts/2025/07/scavenger-malware-distributed-via-eslint-config-prettier-npm-package-supply-chain-compromise/ • https://safedep.io/eslint-config-prettier-major-npm-supply-chain-hack/ • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/supply-chain-security-alert-eslint-config-prettier-package-shows-signs-of-compromise • https://www.crowdstrike.com/en-us/blog/crowdstrike-falcon-prevents-npm-package-supply-chain-attacks/
ATT&CKT1218.011 T1195.002 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer manually testing native-addon build paths by passing custom DLLs to rundll32 (extremely rare)
  • Internal red-team / purple-team supply-chain simulation exercises
  • Custom enterprise npm package that legitimately ships a same-named DLL (would still be benign worth investigating)
  • Endpoint security product replaying captured cmdlines for tuning
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFolderPath !startswith @"C:\Program Files\" or InitiatingProcessVersionInfoCompanyName !has_any ("Microsoft Corporation","Node.js Foundation")
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Pull the full process tree on the host — confirm the grandparent is node.exe / npm / yarn / pnpm and that ProcessCommandLine matches rundll32 *node-gyp.dll*. Hash the DLL and compare against the corroborated Scavenger Loader SHA-256s (90291a2c..., 9ec86514..., c68e42f4..., dd4c4ee2...). Step 2 (contain): Isolate the host from network. Treat any developer / CI npm token, ssh key, and browser-saved credential stored on that host as compromised — rotate immediately. Quarantine the node_modules tree. Step 3 (hunt): Across the fleet, pivot on DeviceFileEvents for node-gyp.dll under node_modules\{eslint-config-prettier,eslint-plugin-prettier,synckit,@pkgr/core,napi-postinstall,got-fetch,is}; check DNS for the Scavenger C2 list (UC2); audit your private registry / package-lock.json / yarn.lock / pnpm-lock.yaml repos for the seven vulnerable version pins.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName =~ "rundll32.exe"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "node-gyp.dll"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","npm.exe","yarn.exe","pnpm.exe","cmd.exe")
    or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("install.js","npm-cli.js","npm.cmd"," yarn "," pnpm ","node_modules")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName,
          ChildCmd = ProcessCommandLine,
          ParentImage = InitiatingProcessFileName,
          ParentCmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          GrandparentImage = InitiatingProcessParentFileName,
          SHA256, FolderPath
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where NewProcessName endswith @"\rundll32.exe"
| where CommandLine has "node-gyp.dll"
| where ParentProcessName has_any (@"\node.exe", @"\npm.exe", @"\yarn.exe", @"\pnpm.exe", @"\cmd.exe")
    or CommandLine has_any ("install.js","npm-cli.js","node_modules")
| where SubjectUserName !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account = SubjectUserName,
          ChildCmd = CommandLine,
          ParentImage = ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Scavenger Loader rundll32 Loading node-gyp.dll (CVE-2025-54313)
id: 7a3f1d4e-6b2c-4a9f-8e7d-1c5b9a2e8d3f
description: Detects the Scavenger Stealer / CVE-2025-54313 supply-chain payload — rundll32.exe loading node-gyp.dll from a compromised npm package install script.
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/maintainers-of-eslint-prettier-plugin-attacked-via-npm-supply-chain-malware/
  - https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-54313
  - https://invokere.com/posts/2025/07/scavenger-malware-distributed-via-eslint-config-prettier-npm-package-supply-chain-compromise/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2025/07/22
tags:
  - attack.t1218.011
  - attack.t1195.002
  - attack.execution
logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: windows
detection:
  selection:
    Image|endswith: '\rundll32.exe'
    CommandLine|contains: 'node-gyp.dll'
  condition: selection
falsepositives:
  - None known — legitimate node-gyp is not loaded via rundll32.
level: high
source:(windows.sysmon OR windows.security) (@EventID:1 OR @EventID:4688) @Image:(*\\rundll32.exe OR *\\RunDll32.exe OR *\\RUNDLL32.EXE) @CommandLine:(*node-gyp.dll* OR *Node-Gyp.dll* OR *NODE-GYP.DLL*)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ImageFileName=/\\rundll32\.exe$/i CommandLine=/node-gyp\.dll/i
| ParentBaseFileName=/^(node|npm|yarn|pnpm|cmd)\.exe$/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, UserName, CommandLine, ParentBaseFileName, ParentImageFileName, SHA256HashData], function=count())
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats summariesonly=true count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where Processes.process_name="rundll32.exe" Processes.process="*node-gyp.dll*" (Processes.parent_process_name="node.exe" OR Processes.parent_process_name="npm.exe" OR Processes.parent_process_name="yarn.exe" OR Processes.parent_process_name="pnpm.exe" OR Processes.parent_process="*install.js*" OR Processes.parent_process="*npm-cli.js*") by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.process_name Processes.process Processes.parent_process_name Processes.parent_process Processes.process_hash | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | `security_content_ctime(firstTime)` | `security_content_ctime(lastTime)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where Processes.process_name="rundll32.exe" Processes.process="*node-gyp.dll*" (Processes.parent_process_name="node.exe" OR Processes.parent_process_name="npm.exe" OR Processes.parent_process_name="yarn.exe" OR Processes.parent_process_name="pnpm.exe" OR Processes.parent_process="*install.js*" OR Processes.parent_process="*npm-cli.js*") by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.process_name Processes.process Processes.parent_process_name Processes.parent_process Processes.process_hash | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | `security_content_ctime(firstTime)` | `security_content_ctime(lastTime)`
Scavenger Stealer C2 beacon to corroborated infrastructure (datahog.su / datalytica.su / smartscreen-api.com) AICommand & Control High
Detects outbound DNS or network connections to the six Scavenger Stealer C2 domains corroborated across Invoke-RE, SafeDep, and CrowdStrike Falcon write-ups. Any contact from an endpoint to this set is post-execution evidence that the npm payload reached its second stage and is exfiltrating .npmrc tokens / browser data. Rationale: Domain list is the verbatim IOC set published by Invoke-RE and mirrored in the public GitHub research repo and StepSecurity / SafeDep advisories. `firebase.su` and `smartscreen-api.com` are typosquats of Google Firebase and Microsoft SmartScreen — no legitimate enterprise reason to resolve them. `.su` TLD is rare enough in modern enterprise traffic that the false-positive surface is near-zero. Cross-checked against: • https://invokere.com/posts/2025/07/scavenger-malware-distributed-via-eslint-config-prettier-npm-package-supply-chain-compromise/ • https://github.com/Invoke-RE/community-malware-research/blob/main/Research/Loaders/Scavenger/IOCs.md • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/supply-chain-security-alert-eslint-config-prettier-package-shows-signs-of-compromise • https://safedep.io/eslint-config-prettier-major-npm-supply-chain-hack/
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1041 T1568
Data sourcesNetwork_Resolution.DNS Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Threat-intel feed validation crawler / VirusTotal hunter resolving the C2 to verify it
  • Security-research lab sandbox detonating samples while reusing the corporate egress
  • Bug-bounty / red-team replaying IOC list against test endpoints
  • Endpoint security agent doing reputation lookup that surfaces the domain in passive DNS
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("msmpeng.exe","smartscreen.exe","securityhealthservice.exe")
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Identify the InitiatingProcessFileName making the lookup — confirm it is rundll32.exe / node.exe / a browser process and inspect the parent chain for npm install activity. Cross-reference with UC1 hits on the same DeviceId. Step 2 (contain): Network-isolate the host immediately. Block all six C2 domains at the DNS / proxy / firewall layer org-wide. Force-rotate the user's npm token, GitHub PAT, cloud credentials, and any browser-saved passwords. Step 3 (hunt): Pivot back 60 days for any other host that resolved these domains. Search for the second-stage stealer DLLs (version.dll/umpdc.dll/profapi.dll dropped outside their usual System32 location). Pull resolved IPs and add to firewall blocklist.
let ScavengerC2 = dynamic(["datahog.su","datalytica.su","datacrab-analytics.com","dieorsuffer.com","firebase.su","smartscreen-api.com"]);
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(60d)
| extend Host = tolower(tostring(parse_url(RemoteUrl).Host))
| where Host has_any (ScavengerC2)
    or RemoteUrl has_any (ScavengerC2)
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName = InitiatingProcessAccountName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Protocol
| order by Timestamp desc
let ScavengerC2 = dynamic(["datahog.su","datalytica.su","datacrab-analytics.com","dieorsuffer.com","firebase.su","smartscreen-api.com"]);
union isfuzzy=true
    (ImDnsActivity
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(60d)
        | where DnsQuery has_any (ScavengerC2)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, SrcUsername, Indicator = DnsQuery, Kind = "dns"),
    (ImNetworkSession
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(60d)
        | where (isnotempty(Url) and Url has_any (ScavengerC2))
             or (isnotempty(DstHostname) and DstHostname has_any (ScavengerC2))
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, SrcUsername, Indicator = coalesce(Url, DstHostname), Kind = "network")
| order by TimeGenerated desc

      - 'datahog.su'

npm registry typosquat npnjs.com — DNS / URL click (eslint-config-prettier maintainer phishing kit) AIDelivery High
Detects DNS lookups, URL clicks, or web sessions to npnjs.com — the typosquat domain used in the spear-phishing email (spoofing support@npmjs.org) that harvested the npm maintainer credentials behind CVE-2025-54313. Hits here are pre-compromise evidence that an internal maintainer or developer is in the kill chain. Rationale: npnjs.com is the specific typosquat domain named in the Snyk article (a single-character substitution of npmjs.com — `m` → `n`). Cross-confirmed by BleepingComputer, CSO Online, and SafeDep coverage of the JounQin maintainer compromise. The domain has no legitimate purpose; any resolution from inside the org is a direct indicator the phishing kit reached a developer mailbox. Cross-checked against: • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/popular-npm-linter-packages-hijacked-via-phishing-to-drop-malware/ • https://www.csoonline.com/article/4026380/prettier-eslint-npm-packages-hijacked-in-a-sophisticated-supply-chain-attack.html • https://safedep.io/eslint-config-prettier-major-npm-supply-chain-hack/
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1583.001 T1195.002
Data sourcesNetwork_Resolution.DNS Web.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Internal threat-intel analyst manually browsing the domain for triage
  • Security awareness training platform using the real phishing URL in a simulated test
  • Sandbox / EDR vendor automated IOC validation
  • Threat hunter pivoting from this rule and re-resolving the domain
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Identify the user / mailbox associated with the lookup or click — if Workload=Email, pull the inbound message via NetworkMessageId. Inspect sender (likely spoofed as support@npmjs.org) and any credential harvesting page contents. Step 2 (contain): Force npm token reset for the targeted user (npm token revoke + npm token create), reset their npm.com account password and enable 2FA, and rotate any GitHub PAT linked to their npm publish workflow. Add npnjs.com to mail-filter block list. Step 3 (hunt): Search EmailUrlInfo / EmailEvents over 90 days for any inbound mail containing npnjs.com or the spoofed support@npmjs.org sender. For every recipient, run UC1 and UC2 on their dev host to confirm no payload landed. Audit all packages published by the user since the click.
union isfuzzy=true
    (UrlClickEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(90d)
        | where Url has "npnjs.com"
        | project Timestamp, AccountUpn, Url, Workload, ActionType, Source = "UrlClickEvents"),
    (DeviceNetworkEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(90d)
        | where RemoteUrl has "npnjs.com"
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountUpn = InitiatingProcessAccountUpn, Url = RemoteUrl, Process = InitiatingProcessFileName, Source = "DeviceNetworkEvents"),
    (EmailUrlInfo
        | where Timestamp > ago(90d)
        | where Url has "npnjs.com"
        | project Timestamp, NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain, Source = "EmailUrlInfo")
| order by Timestamp desc
union isfuzzy=true
    (ImDnsActivity
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(90d)
        | where DnsQuery has "npnjs.com"
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, SrcUsername, Indicator = DnsQuery, Kind = "dns"),
    (ImWebSession
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(90d)
        | where Url has "npnjs.com"
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, SrcUsername, Indicator = Url, Kind = "web")
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Scavenger Loader DLL (node-gyp.dll) written inside node_modules of CVE-2025-54313 packages AIDelivery High
Detects the file-write of a node-gyp.dll PE32+ payload inside the node_modules\ tree of any of the seven packages compromised in CVE-2025-54313: eslint-config-prettier, eslint-plugin-prettier, synckit, @pkgr/core, napi-postinstall, got-fetch, is. Legitimate node-gyp ships as JavaScript and Python build helpers — there is no upstream node-gyp.dll. The presence of this PE inside the package directory is deterministic evidence of a compromised tarball. Rationale: Detection anchored on the seven compromised package names from the Snyk article + the unique node-gyp.dll filename that the malicious tarballs ship. The combination (node_modules path component + specific package name + node-gyp.dll filename) is deterministic — there's no legitimate node-gyp.dll in the real npm distribution. SHA-256 hashes of the known Scavenger Loader samples can be fed into the response runbook to confirm. Cross-checked against: • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-54313 • https://invokere.com/posts/2025/07/scavenger-malware-distributed-via-eslint-config-prettier-npm-package-supply-chain-compromise/ • https://github.com/Invoke-RE/community-malware-research/blob/main/Research/Loaders/Scavenger/IOCs.md • https://socket.dev/blog/npm-is-package-hijacked-in-expanding-supply-chain-attack
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1105
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Internal mirror / Artifactory caching a vulnerable version for IR purposes (intentional retention)
  • Forensic image being unpacked to a sandbox under the same path tree
  • CI build container retaining a malicious node_modules from a pre-fix build
  • Developer extracting the malicious tarball from a vendor IOC bundle for analysis
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("forensic-tool.exe","7zg.exe","7z.exe") or InitiatingProcessFolderPath !contains @"\IR\"
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Hash the dropped node-gyp.dll and compare to the corroborated Scavenger Loader SHA-256s (90291a2c..., 9ec86514..., c68e42f4..., dd4c4ee2...). Read package.json in the same folder to confirm the exact version pin (e.g. eslint-config-prettier@10.1.7). Step 2 (contain): Quarantine node_modules and any build artefacts produced from it. Pull deployment artifacts and CI cache layers that consumed this build. If the host is a developer workstation, treat .npmrc tokens, ssh keys, and saved browser credentials as exfiltrated and rotate. Step 3 (hunt): Search package-lock.json / yarn.lock / pnpm-lock.yaml across all repos for the seven vulnerable version pins (eslint-config-prettier 8.10.1/9.1.1/10.1.6/10.1.7, eslint-plugin-prettier 4.2.2/4.2.3, synckit 0.11.9, @pkgr/core 0.2.8, napi-postinstall 0.3.1, got-fetch 5.1.11/5.1.12, is 3.3.1/5.0.0). Pivot to UC1 and UC2 on every host where node-gyp.dll was found.
let CompromisedPkgPaths = dynamic([
    @"\node_modules\eslint-config-prettier\",
    @"\node_modules\eslint-plugin-prettier\",
    @"\node_modules\synckit\",
    @"\node_modules\@pkgr\core\",
    @"\node_modules\napi-postinstall\",
    @"\node_modules\got-fetch\",
    @"\node_modules\is\"
]);
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(60d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where FileName =~ "node-gyp.dll"
| where FolderPath has_any (CompromisedPkgPaths)
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName = InitiatingProcessAccountName,
          FolderPath, FileName, SHA256, FileSize,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessParentFileName
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(60d)
| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where TargetFileName =~ "node-gyp.dll"
| where TargetFilePath has "node_modules"
| where TargetFilePath has_any ("eslint-config-prettier","eslint-plugin-prettier","synckit",@"@pkgr\core","napi-postinstall","got-fetch",@"\node_modules\is\")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername,
          TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256, TargetFileSize,
          ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Scavenger node-gyp.dll Dropped In CVE-2025-54313 npm Package Tree
id: 9d4f7c1a-3e2b-4a8d-91c6-5f8e2a9b3d47
description: Detects node-gyp.dll PE file written under node_modules\ of any of the seven compromised packages in CVE-2025-54313. No legitimate upstream version of node-gyp ships a DLL by this name.
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/maintainers-of-eslint-prettier-plugin-attacked-via-npm-supply-chain-malware/
  - https://invokere.com/posts/2025/07/scavenger-malware-distributed-via-eslint-config-prettier-npm-package-supply-chain-compromise/
  - https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-54313
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2025/07/22
tags:
  - attack.t1195.002
  - attack.initial_access
logsource:
  category: file_event
  product: windows
detection:
  selection_file:
    TargetFilename|endswith: '\node-gyp.dll'
  selection_path:
    TargetFilename|contains:
      - '\node_modules\eslint-config-prettier\'
      - '\node_modules\eslint-plugin-prettier\'
      - '\node_modules\synckit\'
      - '\node_modules\@pkgr\core\'
      - '\node_modules\napi-postinstall\'
      - '\node_modules\got-fetch\'
      - '\node_modules\is\'
  condition: selection_file and selection_path
level: high
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:11 @TargetFilename:(*\\node-gyp.dll OR *\\Node-Gyp.dll OR *\\NODE-GYP.DLL) @TargetFilename:(*\\node_modules\\eslint-config-prettier\\* OR *\\node_modules\\eslint-plugin-prettier\\* OR *\\node_modules\\synckit\\* OR *\\node_modules\\@pkgr\\core\\* OR *\\node_modules\\napi-postinstall\\* OR *\\node_modules\\got-fetch\\* OR *\\node_modules\\is\\*)
#event_simpleName=FileWritten TargetFileName=/\\node-gyp\.dll$/i
| regex("\\\\node_modules\\\\(eslint-config-prettier|eslint-plugin-prettier|synckit|napi-postinstall|got-fetch|is|@pkgr\\\\core)\\\\", field=TargetFileName, flags="i")
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, TargetFileName, ContextImageFileName, SHA256HashData], function=count())
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats summariesonly=true count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem where Filesystem.file_name="node-gyp.dll" Filesystem.file_path="*\\node_modules\\*" (Filesystem.file_path="*\\eslint-config-prettier\\*" OR Filesystem.file_path="*\\eslint-plugin-prettier\\*" OR Filesystem.file_path="*\\synckit\\*" OR Filesystem.file_path="*\\@pkgr\\core\\*" OR Filesystem.file_path="*\\napi-postinstall\\*" OR Filesystem.file_path="*\\got-fetch\\*" OR Filesystem.file_path="*\\node_modules\\is\\*") by Filesystem.dest Filesystem.user Filesystem.file_path Filesystem.file_hash Filesystem.process_name | `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)` | `security_content_ctime(firstTime)` | `security_content_ctime(lastTime)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem where Filesystem.file_name="node-gyp.dll" Filesystem.file_path="*\\node_modules\\*" (Filesystem.file_path="*\\eslint-config-prettier\\*" OR Filesystem.file_path="*\\eslint-plugin-prettier\\*" OR Filesystem.file_path="*\\synckit\\*" OR Filesystem.file_path="*\\@pkgr\\core\\*" OR Filesystem.file_path="*\\napi-postinstall\\*" OR Filesystem.file_path="*\\got-fetch\\*" OR Filesystem.file_path="*\\node_modules\\is\\*") by Filesystem.dest Filesystem.user Filesystem.file_path Filesystem.file_hash Filesystem.process_name | `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)` | `security_content_ctime(firstTime)` | `security_content_ctime(lastTime)`
Scavenger Stealer sandbox-evasion marker file %TEMP%\SCVNGR_VM created AIInstallation High
Detects creation of the SCVNGR_VM marker file in %TEMP% — Scavenger's anti-analysis / VM-detection breadcrumb dropped by the loader during execution. The string is unique to this malware family and is extremely unlikely to appear anywhere else on a corporate endpoint. Rationale: SCVNGR_VM is the unique sandbox-evasion marker filename documented in the Invoke-RE deep-dive — written by the Scavenger DLL on first run. The string is six characters of malware-specific shorthand with no English-word collision; substring presence anywhere on disk is near-zero false-positive. Cross-checked against: • https://invokere.com/posts/2025/07/scavenger-malware-distributed-via-eslint-config-prettier-npm-package-supply-chain-compromise/ • https://github.com/Invoke-RE/community-malware-research/blob/main/Research/Loaders/Scavenger/IOCs.md
ATT&CKT1497 T1036
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Malware sandbox automation processing a Scavenger sample
  • Threat-intel analyst extracting the IOC list from a research write-up into a local notes file
  • DFIR replay of a captured Scavenger infection on an isolated host
  • Security awareness or red-team exercise simulating the IOC
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Confirm the writing process — Scavenger writes the file from a rundll32.exe context loading node-gyp.dll. Capture full file path, parent process chain, and InitiatingProcessSHA256. Step 2 (contain): Network-isolate the host. Treat all npm tokens, GitHub PATs, cloud credentials, and any browser-stored passwords for the logged-on user as compromised — initiate rotation playbook. Quarantine %TEMP%\SCVNGR_VM and any sibling files in %TEMP% from the same writing process (likely the second-stage payloads version.dll / umpdc.dll / profapi.dll). Step 3 (hunt): Across the fleet, run this query for 60 days back. For each hit, pivot to UC1 (rundll32 + node-gyp.dll), UC2 (Scavenger C2), and UC4 (node-gyp.dll under node_modules) on the same DeviceId to map the full chain.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(60d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where FolderPath has "SCVNGR_VM" or FileName has "SCVNGR_VM"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName = InitiatingProcessAccountName,
          FolderPath, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessParentFileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(60d)
| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where TargetFilePath has "SCVNGR_VM" or TargetFileName has "SCVNGR_VM"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername,
          TargetFilePath, TargetFileName,
          ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

              AttachmentFileName = FileName, AttachmentSHA256 = SHA256;

| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)

// OfficeActivity Send/Receive activity + correlate to host process spawn.

let MailWithAttach = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive")
    | where isnotempty(MessageId)
    | project EmailTime = TimeGenerated, MailboxOwnerUPN, Sender,
              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;

| where EventID == 4688

   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("c68e42f416f482d43653f36cd14384270b54b68d6496a8e34ce887687de5b441") or SHA1 in~ ("c68e42f416f482d43653f36cd14384270b54b68d6496a8e34ce887687de5b441") or MD5 in~ ("c68e42f416f482d43653f36cd14384270b54b68d6496a8e34ce887687de5b441")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("c68e42f416f482d43653f36cd14384270b54b68d6496a8e34ce887687de5b441") or SHA1 in~ ("c68e42f416f482d43653f36cd14384270b54b68d6496a8e34ce887687de5b441") or MD5 in~ ("c68e42f416f482d43653f36cd14384270b54b68d6496a8e34ce887687de5b441")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["c68e42f416f482d43653f36cd14384270b54b68d6496a8e34ce887687de5b441"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Snyk
2025-07-21 12 use cases 3 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

Learn about the recent $500K crypto heist caused by a malicious "Solidity Language" extension in Cursor IDE. Understand how compromised IDE extensions and third-party registries pose significant supply chain risks for developers and the broader AI ecosystem.

Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer). → [LLM] Solidity Language Cursor extension known malicious SHA-256 hash present on disk or executed
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Solidity Language malicious Cursor/VS Code extension folder created on disk (solidityai.solidity-* and related)
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Cursor IDE or VS Code spawning PowerShell/WScript from extensions folder (Solidity Language malware chain) · PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Cursor IDE Malware Extension Compromise in $500k Crypto Heist
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] ScreenConnect MSI sideload from lmfao.su (Solidity Language post-exploit RAT install) · Suspicious browser extension installation · File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Egress to Solidity Language Cursor extension C2 infrastructure (angelic.su / lmfao.su / staketree.net / ab498.pythonanywhere.com / 144.172.1 · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Infostealer — non-browser process accessing browser cookie/login DBs
Cursor IDE or VS Code spawning PowerShell/WScript from extensions folder (Solidity Language malware chain) AIExploitation High
Detects Cursor.exe or Code.exe spawning interpreters (powershell.exe/wscript.exe/cscript.exe/cmd.exe) where the child command line references the Kaspersky-confirmed C2 hosts angelic.su, lmfao.su, staketree.net, paste.ee or ab498.pythonanywhere.com, or where the parent chain is rooted in the .cursor\extensions tree. Anchors directly on the Solidity Language extension.js → PowerShell pivot Kaspersky documented. Rationale: Anchored on Kaspersky-documented chain: Cursor.exe loads `%userprofile%\.cursor\extensions\solidityai.solidity-1.0.9-universal\src\extension.js` which spawns PowerShell to fetch angelic.su/files/1.txt and 2.txt, then staketree.net for the npm-side variant. The combination of IDE parent + scripting child + named C2 host is the high-fidelity tuple. Confirmed by Kaspersky Securelist, Kaspersky corporate blog, and BleepingComputer. Cross-checked against: • https://securelist.com/open-source-package-for-cursor-ai-turned-into-a-crypto-heist/116908/ • https://www.kaspersky.com/blog/malicious-extensions-for-cursor-ai/53802/ • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/malicious-vscode-extension-in-cursor-ide-led-to-500k-crypto-theft/
ATT&CKT1059.001 T1195.002 T1204.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate VS Code/Cursor terminal sessions where the developer pastes commands containing one of these literal domain strings (e.g. reading this very Snyk article in a terminal)
  • Cursor MCP servers or extensions that legitimately invoke PowerShell for build tasks via tasks.json
  • Security researchers running test installations of the malicious extension in a sandbox VM that is also shipping telemetry
  • Cursor auto-update mechanisms that spawn cmd.exe from the extensions directory during plugin refresh
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "--type=extensionHost" and ProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm install","yarn install","node_modules") and not ProcessCommandLine has_any ("angelic.su","lmfao.su","staketree.net","paste.ee/d/","ab498.pythonanywhere.com"))
Response runbook
1. TRIAGE: Pull the InitiatingProcessCommandLine, then list all files written by both parent and child PIDs in DeviceFileEvents in the surrounding 5 minutes; confirm presence of %userprofile%\.cursor\extensions\solidityai.solidity-*; collect hashes and compare to the six Kaspersky SHA-256s. 2. CONTAIN: Isolate the host in Defender (`Isolate-MdeDevice`), kill Cursor.exe + any descendant powershell/wscript, force-block the SHA-256s organisation-wide, and rotate every developer credential and crypto-wallet keystore present on the box. 3. HUNT: Sweep org for `solidityai.*`, `solaibot`, `among-eth`, `blankebesxstnion`, `pythonformat`, `C++ Playground`, `HTTP Format` extension folders, and for any host that resolved angelic.su / lmfao.su / staketree.net / ab498.pythonanywhere.com in DeviceNetworkEvents in the last 90 days.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("Cursor.exe","Code.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","cmd.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("angelic.su","lmfao.su","staketree.net","paste.ee/d/","ab498.pythonanywhere.com")
   or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any (@"\.cursor\extensions\", @"\.vscode\extensions\")
   or InitiatingProcessFolderPath has_any (@"\.cursor\", @"\.vscode\")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName,
          ParentImage = InitiatingProcessFolderPath,
          ParentCmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          ChildImage = FolderPath,
          ChildCmd = ProcessCommandLine,
          SHA256, InitiatingProcessSHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName endswith "\\Cursor.exe" or ParentProcessName endswith "\\Code.exe"
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\\powershell.exe","\\pwsh.exe","\\wscript.exe","\\cscript.exe","\\cmd.exe")
| where CommandLine has_any ("angelic.su","lmfao.su","staketree.net","paste.ee/d/","ab498.pythonanywhere.com",@".cursor\extensions",@".vscode\extensions")
| where SubjectUserName !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, SubjectUserName, ParentProcessName, NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

description: Cursor.exe or Code.exe spawning a script interpreter whose command line references C2 hosts confirmed in the Kaspersky Solidity Language Open VSX malware investigation (angelic.su / lmfao.su / staketree.net / paste.ee / ab498.pythonanywhere.com) or originates from the extensions install tree.
references:

Egress to Solidity Language Cursor extension C2 infrastructure (angelic.su / lmfao.su / staketree.net / ab498.pythonanywhere.com / 144.172.1 AICommand & Control Medium
Network connections or DNS lookups to the C2 hosts Kaspersky published for the Solidity Language Open VSX extension campaign that stole $500K. Direct IOC alert — these domains have no legitimate business use. Rationale: All five hostnames and the IP 144.172.112.84 are vendor-confirmed atomic IOCs from the Kaspersky Securelist write-up. No legitimate enterprise use case for `lmfao.su` or `angelic.su`, and `ab498.pythonanywhere.com` is a single-tenant PythonAnywhere C2 the same actor reused. IP corroborated as the resolver for relay.lmfao.su. Cross-checked against: • https://securelist.com/open-source-package-for-cursor-ai-turned-into-a-crypto-heist/116908/ • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/malicious-vscode-extension-in-cursor-ide-led-to-500k-crypto-theft/ • https://advisory.eventussecurity.com/advisory/fake-solidity-extension-delivers-malware-to-blockchain-developers/
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1568
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic Web.Web Network_Resolution.DNS
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Threat-intel platform automated IOC sync that fetches the indicator list from public sources (legitimate but noisy)
  • Sandboxed malware analysis VMs that intentionally resolve these C2s
  • Security researchers visiting securelist.com or BleepingComputer articles that embed the IOC strings as clickable links (DNS preconnect)
  • SIEM lookup-data ingestion processes that perform DNS validation on indicator feeds
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","SenseIR.exe") or InitiatingProcessFolderPath has @"\Windows Defender Advanced Threat Protection\")
Response runbook
1. TRIAGE: Identify the resolving process via ContextImageFileName/InitiatingProcessFileName; correlate to UC1 (IDE→PowerShell) within ±10 min on the same host; capture the PowerShell command line, scheduled tasks, and any newly created services. 2. CONTAIN: Sinkhole all five domains and 144.172.112.84 at the resolver / next-gen firewall; isolate the originating host; revoke developer SSH keys, AWS/Azure dev tokens, and crypto-wallet seed phrases stored on disk. 3. HUNT: Pivot on the originating SHA256 against the wider fleet; check Email and IM for the same indicators (potential lure); enumerate every host with a `.cursor\extensions\solidityai*` folder via DeviceFileEvents.
let badHosts = dynamic(["angelic.su","relay.lmfao.su","lmfao.su","staketree.net","ab498.pythonanywhere.com"]);
let badIps = dynamic(["144.172.112.84"]);
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteUrl has_any (badHosts) or RemoteIP in (badIps) or RemoteUrl endswith ".angelic.su" or RemoteUrl endswith ".lmfao.su" or RemoteUrl endswith ".staketree.net"

Solidity Language malicious Cursor/VS Code extension folder created on disk (solidityai.solidity-* and related) AIDelivery High
File-event detection for the installation of any extension folder Kaspersky tied to this campaign — solidityai.solidity-*, solaibot, among-eth, blankebesxstnion, pythonformat, C++ Playground, HTTP Format — under %userprofile%\.cursor\extensions\ or %userprofile%\.vscode\extensions\, and especially when extension.js is written inside. Rationale: Kaspersky identifies the exact extension folder name `%userprofile%\.cursor\extensions\solidityai.solidity-1.0.9-universal\src\extension.js` and links sister VS Code marketplace extensions `solaibot`, `among-eth`, `blankebesxstnion` to the same actor by shared C2. Folder-name match is a near-zero-FP signal because no legitimate publisher uses these IDs. Cross-checked against: • https://securelist.com/open-source-package-for-cursor-ai-turned-into-a-crypto-heist/116908/ • https://www.kaspersky.com/blog/malicious-extensions-for-cursor-ai/53802/ • https://www.scworld.com/news/fake-visual-studio-code-extension-for-cursor-led-to-500k-theft
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1176
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • SOC analyst or DFIR engineer manually copying a recovered malicious extension folder to a forensic workstation for analysis
  • Security training environments demoing the IOC to developers
  • File-system backups/restores that recreate historical user profiles containing the artefact
  • Endpoint search/index services touching the folder after the fact and emitting a write event
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("SenseIR.exe","MsMpEng.exe","explorer.exe") and InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("defender","quarantine","forensic"))
Response runbook
1. TRIAGE: Capture full folder listing, hash every file in the extension directory and compare to the six published SHA-256s; pull the InitiatingProcessFileName that wrote it (expect Cursor.exe / Code.exe / VSIX installer). 2. CONTAIN: Force-uninstall the extension (`code --uninstall-extension <id>` then delete folder), isolate host, rotate developer secrets (npm token, GitHub PAT, AWS keys, cloud wallet seeds), block the publisher ID in any in-house extension allow-list. 3. HUNT: Sweep DeviceFileEvents for the same folder names across all developer endpoints in the last 90 days; query the Open VSX / VS Code Marketplace audit logs for who else in the org has these installed; pivot to UC2 to find post-install C2 callbacks.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified")
| where (FolderPath has @"\.cursor\extensions\" or FolderPath has @"\.vscode\extensions\")
| where FolderPath has_any ("solidityai.solidity-","solaibot","among-eth","blankebesxstnion","pythonformat","cppplayground","c++ playground","httpformat","http format")
   or (FileName =~ "extension.js" and FolderPath has_any ("solidityai.solidity-","solaibot","among-eth","blankebesxstnion","pythonformat"))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256, FileSize
| order by Timestamp desc
