| order by TimeGenerated desc

ScreenConnect MSI sideload from lmfao.su (Solidity Language post-exploit RAT install) AIInstallation High
Detects the second-stage MSI install Kaspersky documented — msiexec or curl/Invoke-WebRequest pulling ScreenConnect.ClientSetup.msi from lmfao.su with Access/Guest URL parameters, then ScreenConnect.ClientService.exe joining relay.lmfao.su. This is the threat actor's RAT-of-choice for follow-on hands-on-keyboard activity. Rationale: The URL `https://lmfao.su/Bin/ScreenConnect.ClientSetup.msi?e=Access&y=Guest` is the exact second-stage RAT installer Kaspersky captured. The query-string `?e=Access&y=Guest` is a ScreenConnect-relay-build artefact unique to this actor — it ties any host that runs the installer to this campaign, even on rebuilt SCC binaries. Cross-checked against: • https://securelist.com/open-source-package-for-cursor-ai-turned-into-a-crypto-heist/116908/ • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/malicious-vscode-extension-in-cursor-ide-led-to-500k-crypto-theft/
ATT&CKT1219 T1105
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Web.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • IT helpdesks legitimately running ConnectWise ScreenConnect installs (URL host will be the org's real SCC relay, NOT lmfao.su)
  • Sandbox detonations of the malware sample
  • Threat-intel research workstations curling the IOC URL for verification
  • Sysadmins testing ScreenConnect deployment scripts in a lab using a public sample
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFileName == "ScreenConnect.WindowsClient.exe" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("&h=instance-","&p=8041") and not InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "lmfao.su")
Response runbook
1. TRIAGE: Verify the parent process of msiexec/powershell — expect Cursor.exe descendant chain; capture the resulting ScreenConnect.ClientService.exe install path under %ProgramFiles(x86)%; query the SCC client config (Connect.URL) and confirm it points to relay.lmfao.su / 144.172.112.84. 2. CONTAIN: Stop and uninstall ScreenConnect client (`msiexec /x {GUID}` or registry-driven uninstall), kill running ScreenConnect.ClientService.exe, isolate host, force-block ScreenConnect.ClientSetup.msi by hash org-wide, treat host as fully compromised (rotate ALL secrets accessed by the developer). 3. HUNT: Search DeviceProcessEvents fleet-wide for ScreenConnect.ClientService.exe with parent_cmd containing 144.172.112.84 or relay.lmfao.su; review any AnyDesk/Quasar artefacts dropped alongside; check email/comms apps for the lure that pointed the developer to Open VSX.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName in~ ("msiexec.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","curl.exe","certutil.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine has "lmfao.su"
   or ProcessCommandLine has "ScreenConnect.ClientSetup.msi" and ProcessCommandLine has_any ("e=Access&y=Guest","lmfao")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          ParentName = InitiatingProcessFileName, ParentCmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where NewProcessName endswith_cs "\\msiexec.exe" or NewProcessName endswith_cs "\\powershell.exe" or NewProcessName endswith_cs "\\pwsh.exe" or NewProcessName endswith_cs "\\curl.exe" or NewProcessName endswith_cs "\\certutil.exe" or NewProcessName endswith_cs "\\bitsadmin.exe"
| where CommandLine has "lmfao.su" or (CommandLine has "ScreenConnect.ClientSetup.msi" and CommandLine has_any ("e=Access&y=Guest","lmfao"))
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, SubjectUserName, ParentProcessName, NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Solidity Language Cursor extension known malicious SHA-256 hash present on disk or executed AIWeaponization Medium
Direct hash IOC match against the six SHA-256s Kaspersky published for the Solidity Language Cursor extension supply-chain attack. Hits across process execution, file write, image load, and Defender alert evidence. Rationale: Six SHA-256s published in the Kaspersky Securelist write-up cover the malicious extension.js, the dropped VBScript loaders (a.vbs/b.vbs/m.vbs), and the PureLogs/Quasar payload binaries. Hash matches across DeviceProcessEvents, DeviceFileEvents, and DeviceImageLoadEvents give the broadest catch surface with effectively zero false-positive risk. Cross-checked against: • https://securelist.com/open-source-package-for-cursor-ai-turned-into-a-crypto-heist/116908/ • https://www.kaspersky.com/blog/malicious-extensions-for-cursor-ai/53802/

Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Endpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Malware analyst workstation deliberately handling the sample for triage
  • Threat-intel feed downloading the public sample as part of ingestion
  • Backup restore operations bringing back a quarantined sample from cold storage
  • Cyber-range training exercises distributing the sample to trainees
Response runbook
1. TRIAGE: Determine which of the six hashes hit and from which table (process exec = highest urgency; file-only = possibly historic / quarantined). Cross-reference the host against UC2 (C2 egress) and UC3 (extension folder) within ±24h for full chain confirmation. 2. CONTAIN: Add all six hashes to the Defender allow/block list as Block-and-Quarantine; isolate every host returning a process-exec match; rotate ALL developer secrets, browser-stored credentials, and any crypto-wallet keystores discoverable on the box. 3. HUNT: Pivot on hash-matching hosts back to the originating Open VSX extension publisher (`solidityai.*`, `solaibot`, `among-eth`, `blankebesxstnion`); query the org's web proxy for any access to open-vsx.org publisher pages for those names in the last 180 days to scope total exposure.

Suspicious browser extension installation Installation Medium
Side-loaded / unsigned extensions, often masquerading as wallets, productivity tools.
ATT&CKT1176
Data sourcesEndpoint.Registry DeviceRegistryEvents
DeviceRegistryEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where RegistryKey has_any ("\Software\Google\Chrome\Extensions\","\Software\Microsoft\Edge\Extensions\","\Software\Mozilla\Firefox\Extensions\")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, RegistryKey, RegistryValueName, RegistryValueData,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessAccountName
ImRegistryEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where RegistryKey has_any (@"\Software\Google\Chrome\Extensions\",
                              @"\Software\Microsoft\Edge\Extensions\",
                              @"\Software\Mozilla\Firefox\Extensions\")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, RegistryKey,
          RegistryValue, RegistryValueData, ActingProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Browser Extension Installation via Registry
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d0a
status: experimental
description: |
    Registry writes under the browser extension policy keys —
    administrative install of an extension. Often used by adware /
    info-stealers to bypass user-level extension controls.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1176/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1176
    - attack.persistence
logsource:
    category: registry_event
    product: windows
detection:
    selection:
        TargetObject|contains:
            - '\Software\Google\Chrome\Extensions\'
            - '\Software\Microsoft\Edge\Extensions\'
            - '\Software\Mozilla\Firefox\Extensions\'
    condition: selection
falsepositives:
    - Group-policy-managed extension whitelists
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.

    from datamodel=Endpoint.Registry
    where (Registry.registry_path="*\Software\Google\Chrome\Extensions\*"
        OR Registry.registry_path="*\Software\Microsoft\Edge\Extensions\*"
        OR Registry.registry_path="*\Software\Mozilla\Firefox\Extensions\*")
    by Registry.dest, Registry.registry_path, Registry.registry_value_data, Registry.registry_value_name, Registry.user
| `drop_dm_object_name(Registry)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Registry
    where (Registry.registry_path="*\Software\Google\Chrome\Extensions\*"
        OR Registry.registry_path="*\Software\Microsoft\Edge\Extensions\*"
        OR Registry.registry_path="*\Software\Mozilla\Firefox\Extensions\*")
    by Registry.dest, Registry.registry_path, Registry.registry_value_data, Registry.registry_value_name, Registry.user

Infostealer — non-browser process accessing browser cookie/login DBs Actions on Objectives High
Stealers (RedLine, Lumma, Vidar, Atomic) read Login Data / cookies SQLite from Chrome/Edge/Firefox.
ATT&CKT1539 T1555.003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where FolderPath has_any (@"\Google\Chrome\User Data\", @"\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\", @"\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\")
| where FileName in~ ("Login Data","Cookies","logins.json","cookies.sqlite")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe","brave.exe","opera.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FolderPath, FileName, ActionType

// ASIM ImFileEvent — covers MDE, Sysmon, AuditD, Defender for Cloud, etc.
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where TargetFilePath has_any (@"\Google\Chrome\User Data\",
                                  @"\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\",
                                  @"\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\")
| where TargetFileName in~ ("Login Data","Cookies","logins.json","cookies.sqlite")
| where ActingProcessName !in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe","brave.exe","opera.exe")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName,
          TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, EventType

title: Infostealer - Non-Browser Process Accessing Browser Credential Stores
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d01
status: experimental
description: |
    Stealers (RedLine, Lumma, Vidar, Atomic) read Login Data / cookies
    SQLite databases from Chrome/Edge/Firefox profile directories. Any
    non-browser process touching these files is a high-fidelity signal.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1539/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1555/003/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1539
    - attack.t1555.003
    - attack.credential-access
logsource:
    category: file_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_path:
        TargetFilename|contains:
            - '\Google\Chrome\User Data\'
            - '\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\'
            - '\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\'
    selection_file:
        TargetFilename|endswith:
            - '\Login Data'
            - '\Cookies'
            - '\logins.json'
            - '\cookies.sqlite'
    filter_browser:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\chrome.exe'
            - '\msedge.exe'
            - '\firefox.exe'
            - '\brave.exe'
            - '\opera.exe'
    condition: selection_path and selection_file and not filter_browser
falsepositives:
    - Backup utilities reading user profile files
    - Browser sync agents from non-stock vendors
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where (Filesystem.file_path="*\Google\Chrome\User Data\*\Login Data*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Google\Chrome\User Data\*\Cookies*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\*\Login Data*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\*\logins.json*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\*\cookies.sqlite*")
      AND NOT Filesystem.process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe","brave.exe","opera.exe")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.process_name, Filesystem.file_path, Filesystem.user
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where (Filesystem.file_path="*\Google\Chrome\User Data\*\Login Data*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Google\Chrome\User Data\*\Cookies*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\*\Login Data*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\*\logins.json*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\*\cookies.sqlite*")
      AND NOT Filesystem.process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe","brave.exe","opera.exe")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.process_name, Filesystem.file_path, Filesystem.user
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command Exploitation High
Encoded or obfuscated PowerShell — common across loaders, recon, and post-exploitation.
ATT&CKT1059.001 T1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(-enc|encodedcommand|frombase64string|-nop|-w\s+hidden|invoke-expression|iex\s*\(|downloadstring|net\.webclient)"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\pwsh.exe"
| where CommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(-enc|encodedcommand|frombase64string|-nop|-w\s+hidden|invoke-expression|iex\s*\(|downloadstring|net\.webclient)"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName,
          CommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Obfuscated / Encoded PowerShell Execution
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d08
status: experimental
description: |
    PowerShell launched with -EncodedCommand, base64 strings, hidden
    window, no-profile, bypass policy, IEX / Invoke-Expression / Net
    .WebClient.DownloadString — combinations indicating evasion.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/001/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1027/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059.001
    - attack.t1027
    - attack.execution
    - attack.defense-evasion
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_image:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
    selection_args:
        CommandLine|contains:
            - '-enc '
            - 'EncodedCommand'
            - 'FromBase64String'
            - 'Invoke-Expression'
            - 'IEX('
            - 'DownloadString'
            - 'Net.WebClient'
            - '-w hidden'
            - '-WindowStyle Hidden'
            - '-nop'
            - '-NoProfile'
            - '-ep bypass'
            - '-ExecutionPolicy Bypass'
    condition: selection_image and selection_args
falsepositives:
    - DevOps tooling that legitimately uses encoded command for
      cross-shell escaping
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe")
      AND (Processes.process="*-enc *" OR Processes.process="*EncodedCommand*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64String*" OR Processes.process="*-nop*"
        OR Processes.process="*-w hidden*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*IEX(*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*Net.WebClient*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe")
      AND (Processes.process="*-enc *" OR Processes.process="*EncodedCommand*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64String*" OR Processes.process="*-nop*"
        OR Processes.process="*-w hidden*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*IEX(*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*Net.WebClient*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["2c471e265409763024cdc33579c84d88d5aaf9aea1911266b875d3b7604a0eeb", "404dd413f10ccfeea23bfb00b0e403532fa8651bfb456d84b6a16953355a800a", "70309bf3d2aed946bba51fc3eedb2daa3e8044b60151f0b5c1550831fbc6df17", "84d4a4c6d7e55e201b20327ca2068992180d9ec08a6827faa4ff3534b96c3d6f", "eb5b35057dedb235940b2c41da9e3ae0553969f1c89a16e3f66ba6f6005c6fa8", "f4721f32b8d6eb856364327c21ea3c703f1787cfb4c043f87435a8876d903b2c", "ce72b79e324371134db762fe70b8b1789af899d7217461bc3658a6bd84743eb6", "e19d5d8f941b9a98fbb3b65e1e6077fa00d97529e351e455297b0204ec07e9ed", "209fb5bb2440ffe1a631dfe3b574229105a33c5153eded023cc77d8e8f81d1de", "e0ca66c1a9a68b319b24a7c6b8fdca219dffd802dd4de2d59f602c4d90f40d6c", "c5c0228a1e0ba2bb748219325f66acf17078a26165b45728d8e98150377aa068", "a1eadd41327bd8736e275627d3953944fe7089c032d72a3e429ff18ad0958ada", "c3684164933c3f54d5b0b242a8a906a85d633de479079a820bb804c0f73c0f58"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

// Article-specific bespoke detection — Cursor IDE Malware Extension Compromise in $500k Crypto Heist
// Hunts the actual binaries / paths / commandline fragments named
// in the article instead of a generic technique-class template.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where (FileName in~ ("extension.js"))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName,
          FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

// File-creation events for the named binaries / paths
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified")
| where (FileName in~ ("extension.js"))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FolderPath,
          FileName, ActionType, InitiatingProcessFileName,
          InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

Actions on Objectives
—
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

The Consortium for Information and Software Quality estimated that the cost of poor software quality in the United States reached $2.41 trillion in 2022. ATT&CKT1190 T1486
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix
Phase 1
Reconnaissance
—
Phase 2
Weaponization
—
Phase 3
Delivery
—
Phase 4
Exploitation
Detected
Phase 5
Installation
Detected
Phase 6

Likely
Phase 7
Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → RMM tool installed by non-IT user — remote-access utility for hands-on-keyboard
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change · LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) · Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement
Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) Actions on Objectives High
Mimikatz, comsvcs.dll MiniDump, or any non-Windows process opening LSASS.
ATT&CKT1003.001 T1003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceEvents
DeviceEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType == "OpenProcessApiCall"
| where FileName =~ "lsass.exe"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","csrss.exe",
                                          "svchost.exe","wininit.exe","services.exe",
                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
// SecurityEvent EventID 4663 = object access. AccessMask 0x10 / 0x40 / 0x1010
// indicate read access to lsass.exe — credential-dump signature. Audit
// Object Access > Audit File System policy required.
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4663
| where ObjectName endswith "\lsass.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ObjectName, ProcessName,
          AccessMask, AccessReason
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: LSASS Process Access Outside Microsoft-Signed Tooling
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d04
status: experimental
description: |
    Anything opening lsass.exe with read-memory access that isn't a
    Microsoft signed component (Defender, csrss, etc.) is suspect.
    Mimikatz / Pypykatz / nanodump signature.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1003/001/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1003.001
    - attack.credential-access
logsource:
    category: process_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection:
        TargetImage|endswith: '\lsass.exe'
    filter_known_good:
        SourceImage|endswith:
            - '\MsSense.exe'
            - '\MsMpEng.exe'
            - '\csrss.exe'
            - '\svchost.exe'
            - '\wininit.exe'
            - '\services.exe'
            - '\lsm.exe'
            - '\SearchProtocolHost.exe'
    condition: selection and not filter_known_good
falsepositives:
    - Third-party EDR / forensic tools — extend the filter list
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where (Processes.process="*lsass*" OR Processes.process="*sekurlsa*"
        OR Processes.process="*MiniDump*" OR Processes.process="*comsvcs.dll*MiniDump*"
        OR Processes.process="*procdump*lsass*")
       OR (Processes.process_name="rundll32.exe" AND Processes.process="*comsvcs*MiniDump*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where (Processes.process="*lsass*" OR Processes.process="*sekurlsa*"

ATT&CKT1021.002 T1569.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
   or (FileName =~ "wmic.exe" and ProcessCommandLine has "/node:")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by Timestamp desc

SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688            // process-create (audit-policy required)
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\psexec.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\psexesvc.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\paexec.exe"
   or (NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe" and CommandLine has "/node:")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: PsExec / SMB Lateral Movement Tooling Execution
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d03
status: experimental
description: |
    PsExec, paexec, smbexec, csexec, remcom — admin-grade tooling that
    pivots over SMB. Pair with `wmic /node:` for the full cluster.

    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1021/002/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1569/002/

| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.process_name IN ("AnyDesk.exe","TeamViewer.exe","TeamViewer_Service.exe",
        "ScreenConnect.ClientService.exe","ConnectWiseControl.ClientService.exe",
        "atera_agent.exe","SplashtopStreamer.exe","RustDesk.exe","NinjaOne.exe","kaseya*.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name

AI Trust in Action: How Snyk Agent Redefines Secure Development

Snyk
2025-06-02 2 use cases 0 techniques 3 kill-chain phases detected

Discover how Snyk Agent Fix delivers trusted, real-time vulnerability remediation with validated AI fixes built for developers and trusted by security.

Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix

Actions on Objectives
—
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha) Exploitation High
Browser-pasted PowerShell launched from explorer/Run dialog — ClickFix social engineering.
ATT&CKT1204.004 T1059.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("explorer.exe","RuntimeBroker.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","mshta.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(iex|invoke-expression|frombase64|downloadstring|hxxp|curl |wget )"

SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName endswith "\explorer.exe"
   or ParentProcessName endswith "\RuntimeBroker.exe"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\pwsh.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| where CommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(iex|invoke-expression|frombase64|downloadstring|hxxp|curl |wget )"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: FakeCaptcha / ClickFix - Clipboard-Injected PowerShell from Explorer
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d06
status: experimental
description: |
    "FakeCaptcha" / "ClickFix" social-engineering: malicious site asks
    user to press Win+R and paste a payload. The result is
    powershell/mshta launched directly from explorer.exe with iex /
    base64 / downloadstring in the command line.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/001/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1204/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059.001
    - attack.t1204.002
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_parent:
        ParentImage|endswith:
            - '\explorer.exe'
            - '\RuntimeBroker.exe'
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
    selection_cmd:
        CommandLine|contains:
            - 'iex'
            - 'invoke-expression'
            - 'frombase64'
            - 'downloadstring'
            - 'hxxp'
            - 'curl '
            - 'wget '
    condition: selection_parent and selection_child and selection_cmd
falsepositives:
    - IT-driven scripted automation that admins paste-launch
level: high
ENDPOINT

| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("explorer.exe","RuntimeBroker.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","mshta.exe")
      AND (Processes.process="*iex*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*hxxp*" OR Processes.process="*curl*" OR Processes.process="*wget*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium

ATT&CKT1204.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Endpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Registry
// Article-specific bespoke detection — Security Testing for Single-Page Applications (SPAs)
// Hunts the actual binaries / paths / commandline fragments named
// in the article instead of a generic technique-class template.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where (FileName in~ ("vue.js"))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName,
          FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

// File-creation events for the named binaries / paths
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified")
| where (FileName in~ ("vue.js"))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FolderPath,
          FileName, ActionType, InitiatingProcessFileName,
          InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc
NETWORK
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium

CVE-2025-29927 Authorization Bypass in Next.js Middleware

Snyk
2025-03-23 4 use cases 2 techniques 3 kill-chain phases detected

On Friday morning, March 21, 2025, at 9:00 a.m. UTC, a security advisory identified as CVE-2025-29927 was published. It cited a critical 9.1 severity vulnerability for mainstream Next.js applications.

CVEsCVE-2025-29927
ATT&CKT1190 T1195.002
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix
Phase 1
Reconnaissance
Detected
Phase 2
Weaponization
—
Phase 3
Delivery
Detected
Phase 4
Exploitation
Detected
Phase 5
Installation
Likely
Phase 6
Command & Control
Likely
Phase 7
Actions on Objectives
—
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Next.js CVE-2025-29927 middleware bypass via x-middleware-subrequest header · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — CVE-2025-29927 Authorization Bypass in Next.js Middleware
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Next.js CVE-2025-29927 middleware bypass via x-middleware-subrequest header AIExploitation High
Hunts inbound HTTP requests carrying the literal header `x-middleware-subrequest: middleware` against self-hosted Next.js apps. This header is the documented bypass primitive for CVE-2025-29927 — no legitimate external client ever sets it, because it is an internal-only Next.js signal designed to avoid recursive middleware invocation. Any external occurrence is exploitation or recon. Rationale: Detection anchors on the exact header string named in the article and Vercel advisory — `x-middleware-subrequest`. The article's Block 2 verbatim shows `x-middleware-subrequest: middleware`. This header is an internal Next.js framework signal that should never appear in inbound traffic from external clients, making any external occurrence an exploitation attempt. Cross-checked with the Vercel security advisory at https://github.com/vercel/next.js/security/advisories/GHSA-f82v-jwr5-mffw and the GHSA entry which confirm the header is the specific bypass primitive and that version-specific value variants exist (the `has` operator catches them all). Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/vercel/next.js/security/advisories/GHSA-f82v-jwr5-mffw • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-29927 • https://zhero-web-sec.github.io/research-and-things/nextjs-and-the-corrupt-middleware
ATT&CKT1190 T1078 T1556
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Authorized penetration test or red-team engagement exercising the CVE intentionally
  • Vulnerability scanners (Snyk, Nuclei, Burp templates) probing CVE-2025-29927 from a known scanner IP range
  • Internal Next.js subrequest traffic that erroneously transits an external WAF due to misconfigured routing
  • Bug-bounty researcher submitting via your responsible-disclosure inbound IPs
Suppression filter
// Add to WafHits / SyslogHits / IISHits before the union:
// | where SourceIP !in ("<authorized-pentest-IP-1>", "<authorized-pentest-IP-2>")
// | where RequestClientApplication !contains "Nuclei" and RequestClientApplication !contains "Snyk-Scanner"
Response runbook
1. Triage: pull the source IP, request URL, and response status from the matched events. Confirm the target host is running a self-hosted Next.js version in the vulnerable range (11.1.4–13.5.6, 14.x < 14.2.25, 15.x < 15.2.3); deployments on Vercel/Netlify are not affected. Determine whether the matched request reached a protected route (e.g. /admin, /api/*) and what response code it received. 2. Contain: block the offending source IP at the WAF/edge, deploy the Cloudflare managed-WAF rule that strips the header, and roll out the patched Next.js version (14.2.25 / 15.2.3 / 13.5.9). As an interim, add an edge rule that drops `x-middleware-subrequest` on inbound traffic. 3. Hunt: retro-search the last 30 days for the same header from any external IP, correlate with authenticated session creation / privileged-action logs from the same source IP, and review admin-route access logs for anomalous 200 OK responses to unauthenticated principals.
// Defender Advanced Hunting does not natively capture inbound HTTP request headers for self-hosted web servers.
// This detection is implemented on Sentinel/SIEM web-tier telemetry instead — see sentinel_kql.
// For Defender, the closest pivot is host-side: a Next.js node process receiving traffic that is followed by
// post-auth-only commands (DB dumps, secret reads) from an unauthenticated session — too noisy to express here.

// Hunt CVE-2025-29927 exploitation: external clients sending the bypass header to self-hosted Next.js apps.
// Header lives in WAF / reverse-proxy event text. AppServiceHTTPLogs does not surface arbitrary request
// headers, so we cover WAF (CommonSecurityLog) + raw nginx/IIS via Syslog/W3CIISLog where ingested.
let Window = 7d;
// Path 1 — WAF / reverse-proxy events that log full request headers/context
let WafHits = CommonSecurityLog
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(Window)
    | where (RequestContext has "x-middleware-subrequest")
         or (Message has "x-middleware-subrequest")
         or (DeviceCustomString1 has "x-middleware-subrequest")
         or (DeviceCustomString2 has "x-middleware-subrequest")
         or (DeviceCustomString3 has "x-middleware-subrequest")
         or (DeviceCustomString4 has "x-middleware-subrequest")
         or (DeviceCustomString5 has "x-middleware-subrequest")
         or (DeviceCustomString6 has "x-middleware-subrequest")
    | project TimeGenerated, SourceIP, SourceUserName, DeviceVendor, DeviceProduct,
              DestinationHostName, RequestURL, RequestMethod, RequestClientApplication,
              Evidence = coalesce(RequestContext, Message, DeviceCustomString1, DeviceCustomString2, DeviceCustomString3, DeviceCustomString4, DeviceCustomString5, DeviceCustomString6),
              Layer = "WAF";
// Path 2 — raw nginx / Apache access logs forwarded via Syslog (some teams strip headers, some don't)
let SyslogHits = Syslog
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(Window)
    | where SyslogMessage has "x-middleware-subrequest"
    | project TimeGenerated, SourceIP = HostIP, SourceUserName = "", DeviceVendor = "linux", DeviceProduct = Facility,
              DestinationHostName = HostName, RequestURL = "", RequestMethod = "", RequestClientApplication = "",
              Evidence = SyslogMessage, Layer = "Syslog";
// Path 3 — IIS reverse-proxy in front of Next.js (header value sometimes ends up in csUriQuery via URL Rewrite Module)
let IISHits = W3CIISLog
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(Window)
    | where csUriQuery has "x-middleware-subrequest" or csReferer has "x-middleware-subrequest"
    | project TimeGenerated, SourceIP = cIP, SourceUserName = csUserName, DeviceVendor = "iis", DeviceProduct = "W3CIISLog",
              DestinationHostName = Computer, RequestURL = strcat(csUriStem, "?", csUriQuery), RequestMethod = csMethod,
              RequestClientApplication = csUserAgent,
              Evidence = strcat(csUriQuery, " | ", csReferer), Layer = "IIS";
union isfuzzy=true WafHits, SyslogHits, IISHits
| extend SourceIP = tostring(SourceIP)
| summarize Hits = count(), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated),
            SampleEvidence = any(Evidence), Hosts = make_set(DestinationHostName, 25),
            URLs = make_set(RequestURL, 25), UAs = make_set(RequestClientApplication, 10),
            Layers = make_set(Layer, 5)
    by SourceIP
| order by FirstSeen asc
title: Next.js CVE-2025-29927 Middleware Authorization Bypass Header
id: 4f5b7a1c-8e21-4f2a-9d31-2a3c1e9b7a55
status: experimental
description: Detects HTTP requests carrying the x-middleware-subrequest header used to bypass Next.js middleware authorization (CVE-2025-29927). This header is internal-only and should never be present on traffic from external clients.
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/cve-2025-29927-authorization-bypass-in-next-js-middleware/
  - https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-f82v-jwr5-mffw
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2025/03/23
tags:
  - attack.initial_access
  - attack.t1190
  - attack.defense_evasion
  - attack.t1556
  - cve.2025.29927
logsource:
  category: webserver
detection:
  selection_header:
    cs-headers|contains: 'x-middleware-subrequest'
  selection_alt:
    - http_request_headers|contains: 'x-middleware-subrequest'
    - request|contains: 'x-middleware-subrequest: middleware'
  condition: selection_header or selection_alt
falsepositives:
  - Internal Next.js framework-to-framework subrequests if logged at the edge (rare; usually loopback only)
  - Security scanners (Snyk, Burp, Nuclei) running authorized assessments
level: high
(source:nginx OR source:apache OR source:aws.alb OR source:cloudfront OR source:azure.appservice OR source:fastly OR source:cloudflare) (@http.headers:*x-middleware-subrequest* OR @http.request.headers:*x-middleware-subrequest* OR @http.headers.x-middleware-subrequest:* OR @network.headers:*x-middleware-subrequest*)
// Falcon FDR does not capture inbound HTTP request headers from self-hosted web servers.
// If NG-SIEM is ingesting nginx/IIS/ALB logs as third-party sources, pivot there; the FDR-only equivalent
// is the post-bypass host-side outcome (Next.js node spawning unexpected children) which is too generic.
CLOUD
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Article-specific behavioural hunt — Unburdening Developers From Vulnerability Fatigue with Snyk Delta Findings
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.

Reconstructing the TJ Actions Changed Files GitHub Actions Compromise

Snyk
2025-03-17 8 use cases 3 techniques 7 kill-chain phases detected

A critical security exploit in the popular GitHub Action changed-files (tj-actions/changed-files) exposed encrypted secrets in plaintext within GitHub Action logs. This vulnerability, affecting over 23,000 repositories, was enabled by orphaned commits and manipulated release tags. Learn how to protect your GitHub workflows from similar exploits.

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer). → [LLM] Git checkout of compromised tj-actions/changed-files commit on runner host
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Self-hosted GitHub Action runner downloads memdump.py from compromised gist (CVE-2025-30066)

Self-hosted GitHub Action runner downloads memdump.py from compromised gist (CVE-2025-30066) AIDelivery High
Hunts the exact exfil step of the tj-actions/changed-files compromise: a curl/wget on a self-hosted runner reaching the nikitastupin gist that hosted memdump.py. Catches both the canonical gist path and any rehosted copy of memdump.py. Rationale: The gist path /nikitastupin/30e525b776c409e03c2d6f328f254965/raw/memdump.py is hard-coded in the malicious commit (article Block: updateFeatures function). Combining curl/wget with this specific gist ID or 'memdump.py' filename yields near-zero false positives. Cross-checked against Wiz and CISA advisories which both cite this gist as the exclusive secondary stage in CVE-2025-30066. Cross-checked against: • https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/alerts/2025/03/18/supply-chain-compromise-third-party-tj-actionschanged-files-cve-2025-30066-and-reviewdogaction • https://www.wiz.io/blog/github-action-tj-actions-changed-files-supply-chain-attack-cve-2025-30066 • https://www.sysdig.com/blog/detecting-and-mitigating-the-tj-actions-changed-files-supply-chain-attack-cve-2025-30066 • https://github.com/advisories/ghsa-mrrh-fwg8-r2c3
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1105
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researcher fetching the gist for IOC analysis
  • Threat-intel platform automated IOC enrichment job
  • SOC tabletop exercise replaying the attack chain
  • DFIR retro-hunt on archived runner image
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessParentFileName !in~ ("falcon-sensor","mdatp","osqueryd") and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "ioc-enrichment"
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the host is a self-hosted GitHub Action runner; check ProcessCreationTime against the CVE-2025-30066 window (2025-03-12 00:00 → 2025-03-15 12:00 UTC, but assume rehosts after); pull the curl response body and stdout. 2) Contain: revoke ALL repository / org / environment secrets that the runner had access to (GitHub PATs, AWS keys, npm tokens, RSA keys, signing certs); rotate before any commit. Isolate the runner host from the network. 3) Hunt: search audit logs for the runner's outbound network and process events ±2 hours; check the parent workflow YAML for `tj-actions/changed-files` pinning; scan org for other workflows referencing the same action; look for sibling reviewdog/action-setup (CVE-2025-30154) usage in same window.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName in~ ("curl","wget","curl.exe","wget.exe","bash","sh")
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("gist.githubusercontent.com/nikitastupin/30e525b776c409e03c2d6f328f254965","memdump.py","30e525b776c409e03c2d6f328f254965")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessParentFileName, FolderPath
| order by Timestamp desc
// Self-hosted runners on Linux ship via Syslog (auditd) or DeviceProcessEvents if MDE-Linux is shipped to Sentinel
union isfuzzy=true
  (DeviceProcessEvents
   | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
   | where FileName in~ ("curl","wget","bash","sh")
   | where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("gist.githubusercontent.com/nikitastupin/30e525b776c409e03c2d6f328f254965","memdump.py")
   | project TimeGenerated=Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, Cmd=ProcessCommandLine, ParentCmd=InitiatingProcessCommandLine),
  (Syslog
   | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
   | where SyslogMessage has_any ("gist.githubusercontent.com/nikitastupin/30e525b776c409e03c2d6f328f254965","memdump.py")
   | project TimeGenerated, DeviceName=Computer, AccountName="", Cmd=SyslogMessage, ParentCmd="")
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Runner.Worker process memory dumped via /proc/PID/mem read on Linux runner AIActions on Objectives High
Detects the credential-access primitive of CVE-2025-30066: a python process opening /proc/<pid>/maps and /proc/<pid>/mem of the GitHub Actions Runner.Worker to scrape plaintext secrets. Hunts the exact technique memdump.py uses, not the script name. Rationale: The article's reproduced memdump.py opens /proc/<pid>/maps and /proc/<pid>/mem after finding the pid of 'Runner.Worker'. Reads of /proc/PID/mem by python (not gdb/strace/criu) are exceptionally rare in normal CI/CD operation, so a process-creation join with the /proc/PID/mem open is a high-fidelity, attack-specific signal that survives the script being renamed. Verified against Wiz and Sysdig analyses that both call out this primitive. Cross-checked against: • https://www.wiz.io/blog/github-action-tj-actions-changed-files-supply-chain-attack-cve-2025-30066 • https://www.sysdig.com/blog/detecting-and-mitigating-the-tj-actions-changed-files-supply-chain-attack-cve-2025-30066 • https://semgrep.dev/blog/2025/popular-github-action-tj-actionschanged-files-is-compromised/
ATT&CKT1003 T1552.001 T1212
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • GDB or pdb debugger session by a developer (different parent — usually gdb, not python)
  • CRIU checkpoint/restore in container orchestration
  • perf-tools / py-spy profiling (uses /proc/PID/mem too — distinguish via parent and cmdline)
  • SRE incident-response live memory triage with volatility/lime
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has_any ("py-spy","pdb","gdb","criu","volatility","lime") and InitiatingProcessParentFileName !in~ ("gdb","strace","perf")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the reading python's command line and parent — legit profilers like py-spy declare themselves in argv; memdump.py runs from stdin (curl | python3) so cmdline shows only 'python3'. Pull the full process tree back to the runner agent. 2) Contain: kill the python process, isolate the runner, revoke every secret the runner could touch (repo, environment, org, OIDC role assumptions). Snapshot /proc/<pid>/maps output if still resident — it contains the secrets harvested. 3) Hunt: pivot on DeviceId for the curl/wget that staged the script (UC #1), and search GitHub audit logs for workflow_run / push events on this runner in the dump window to enumerate which repos/secrets were exposed.
// Memory dump primitive: python reading /proc/PID/mem (and usually /proc/PID/maps first)
let PythonProcs = DeviceProcessEvents
  | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
  | where FileName matches regex @"^python[23]?$"
  | where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("Runner.Worker","/proc/","memdump.py","isSecret")
  | project Timestamp, DeviceId, DeviceName, AccountName, ProcessId=tostring(ProcessId), ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine;
let MemReads = DeviceFileEvents
  | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
  | where FolderPath startswith "/proc/" and FileName in~ ("mem","maps")
  | where InitiatingProcessFileName matches regex @"^python[23]?$"
  | project Timestamp, DeviceId, DeviceName, FolderPath, FileName, InitiatingProcessId=tostring(InitiatingProcessId), InitiatingProcessCommandLine;
MemReads
| join kind=inner (PythonProcs) on DeviceId, $left.InitiatingProcessId == $right.ProcessId
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FolderPath, FileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, ParentCmd=InitiatingProcessCommandLine1
| order by Timestamp desc
// Auditd path-watch on /proc/*/mem; requires audit rules: -w /proc -p r -k proc_read
Syslog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where Facility =~ "auth" or ProcessName =~ "auditd" or SyslogMessage has "type=PATH"
| where SyslogMessage matches regex @"name=\x22?/proc/[0-9]+/(mem|maps)\x22?"
| where SyslogMessage matches regex @"comm=\x22?python[23]?\x22?"
| extend ProcPath = extract(@"name=\x22?(/proc/[0-9]+/(?:mem|maps))\x22?", 1, SyslogMessage)
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, HostIP, ProcPath, SyslogMessage
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Malicious tj-actions base64 payload prefix observed in process command line AIExploitation High
Pins to the exact 24-character base64 prefix 'aWYgW1sgIiRPU1RZUEUiID09' that decodes to 'if [[ "$OSTYPE" ==' — the literal opening of the malicious blob injected into tj-actions/changed-files. Any execution of bash -c with this prefix is the attack, not a behavioural lookalike. Rationale: The article's verbatim updateFeatures() function passes 'aWYgW1sgIiRPU1RZUEUiID09...' to bash -c — this base64 prefix is the literal payload signature. The grep regex inside the decoded blob targets '\"isSecret\":true' which is unique to GitHub Actions secret JSON structure dumped from Runner.Worker memory. Combined with the public malicious commit SHA (confirmed by Wiz, CISA, Unit 42), three independent string pivots — false-positive ceiling is effectively zero outside DFIR rehearsal. Cross-checked against: • https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/alerts/2025/03/18/supply-chain-compromise-third-party-tj-actionschanged-files-cve-2025-30066-and-reviewdogaction • https://www.wiz.io/blog/github-action-tj-actions-changed-files-supply-chain-attack-cve-2025-30066 • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/github-actions-supply-chain-attack/ • https://github.com/advisories/ghsa-mrrh-fwg8-r2c3

Git checkout of compromised tj-actions/changed-files commit on runner host AIWeaponization Medium
Catches the moment a self-hosted runner (or any CI host) fetches/checks out the malicious tj-actions/changed-files commit SHA 0e58ed8671d6b60d0890c21b07f8835ace038e67, or pins the action to a tag that was re-pointed during the CVE-2025-30066 window. Detects the initial-access foothold before any payload runs. Rationale: The malicious commit SHA 0e58ed8671d6b60d0890c21b07f8835ace038e67 is confirmed by CISA, Wiz, and Sysdig as the exclusive entry point. Re-pointed tags during the CVE-2025-30066 window all resolved to this SHA, so any git checkout / node action loader processing this hash on a runner host is high-confidence evidence of exploitation. The inventory pivot ('tj-actions/changed-files' anywhere) is intentionally noisy — it's the asset-management hunt that finds which workflows still need to be re-pinned to v46.0.1.     A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)

    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.

    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("0e58ed8671d6b60d0890c21b07f8835ace038e67") or SHA1 in~ ("0e58ed8671d6b60d0890c21b07f8835ace038e67") or MD5 in~ ("0e58ed8671d6b60d0890c21b07f8835ace038e67")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("0e58ed8671d6b60d0890c21b07f8835ace038e67") or SHA1 in~ ("0e58ed8671d6b60d0890c21b07f8835ace038e67") or MD5 in~ ("0e58ed8671d6b60d0890c21b07f8835ace038e67")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["0e58ed8671d6b60d0890c21b07f8835ace038e67"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-22869")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-22869")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc
